## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one half (good half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (bad half).
- We need to identify which half is "good" (decreases score) and bring items from there.
- BLACK floor = source area. WHITE floor = nest. GRAY = in between.
- Robots should: go to source, pick up items from the GOOD half, return to nest.
- The good half is behind either BLUE or GREEN walls. Since we can't know which is good a priori, we'll try both.
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK/GRAY/WHITE), wall colors (BLUE=color 3, GREEN=color 2, RED=color 1).

**Color mapping:**
- RED = 1, GREEN = 2, BLUE = 3, YELLOW = 4, MAGENTA = 5, CYAN = 6

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Explore/move toward source (BLACK floor area)
- State 1: When on BLACK floor, go toward one colored wall (GREEN or BLUE) to get good items
- State 2: When carrying item, return to nest (go toward RED wall or white floor)
- State 3: When on WHITE floor, deliver and restart

**Basic 3-state machine:**
1. Explore toward source (GoToColor BLACK-like? No, go away from RED wall)
2. At source, go toward BLUE (or GREEN) wall
3. Return to nest (GoToColor RED wall)

Since we don't know which half is good, I'll try BLUE-seeking and GREEN-seeking variants.

**Transitions:**
- From exploration: if floor is BLACK → go to source behavior
- From source: if floor is WHITE → return to nest (already delivered? No, need to go back)
- Actually: floor BLACK = at source (picked up item), then go toward RED wall to deliver
- Floor WHITE = at nest (delivered), then explore again

Let me design clean FSMs:

**3-state FSM (BLUE half):**
- S0: GoToColor BLUE (vel=1.0), transitions: if WHITE floor → S1, if random → S2
- S1: GoToColor RED (nest return), transitions: if BLACK floor → S0
- S2: Exploration, transitions: if BLACK floor → S0

**2-state FSM:**
- S0: GoToColor BLUE, if WHITE → S1
- S1: GoToColor RED, if BLACK → S0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to make informed decisions. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify further.

## Reasoning

**Key insight:** The mission requires robots to:
1. Navigate from center to the source (BLACK floor)
2. Pick up items from the GOOD half (either BLUE or GREEN wall side)
3. Return to nest (WHITE floor, RED wall side)

The good half decreases score by 1, bad half increases by 1. We don't know which color wall marks the good half - so we need to try both BLUE and GREEN strategies.

**Critical observations:**
- GoAwayColor RED (color 1) naturally pushes robots toward the source
- GoToColor RED naturally pulls robots toward the nest
- GoToColor BLUE (3) or GREEN (2) targets specific source halves
- Floor color (BLACK/GRAY/WHITE) is the key state detector

**Best guesses for good machines:**
- S0: Move away from RED (toward source) → on BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor BLUE or GREEN (get specific item) → on WHITE floor after crossing → S2  
- S2: GoToColor RED (return to nest) → on BLACK → S0

But the issue is robots can't "know" they're carrying an item. They just enter the source half repeatedly. The trick is to spend time in BLACK and then return.

Let me try machines where:
- When on GRAY/exploring, move toward source colors or away from RED
- When on BLACK, briefly stay then return
- Use probability transitions to create oscillation

I'll generate 14 machines mixing BLUE and GREEN targeting, with various topologies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received score feedback from the simulator. I'll reason more carefully and diversify my approach significantly.

## Reasoning

**Re-reading the problem carefully:**
- Score DECREASES by 1 for items from ONE half (good), INCREASES by 1 for items from the OTHER half (bad)
- We want to minimize score, so we want good items delivered
- Robots can't distinguish what they're carrying - they just need to consistently visit the RIGHT half

**Key insight I may have missed:** The robot automatically picks up an item upon entering a source half, replacing any previous item. So the strategy should be:
1. Go to the GOOD half specifically (BLUE or GREEN wall side)
2. Return to nest (WHITE floor / RED wall)
3. Repeat

**The challenge:** Without knowing which half is good, I must hedge. But actually, in the evolutionary process, the simulator will reward one color over the other. Since I'm generating pairs (BLUE vs GREEN), one will score better.

**Better FSM logic:**
- The simplest effective machine: GoToColor(target_wall) → when on WHITE floor → GoToColor(RED) → when on BLACK floor → repeat
- But GoToColor(RED) at nest means staying at nest. Better: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) always, and GoToColor(RED) returns to nest.

**Actually:** GoToColor navigates toward the COLOR in the camera. BLUE=3, GREEN=2, RED=1. The walls behind the source halves are blue or green. The nest wall is RED.

Let me try cleaner designs with higher transition probabilities and explore more topologies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit score feedback. Let me reconsider the problem from scratch more carefully.

## Reasoning

**Critical re-analysis:**

The problem says "an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." We want to MINIMIZE score (make it as negative as possible). So we want robots to repeatedly deliver items from the GOOD half.

**What colors can robots actually see?**
- Floor: BLACK (source), GRAY (middle), WHITE (nest)
- Wall colors: RED (nest wall), BLUE/GREEN (source wall halves)
- The `GoToColor` behavior uses the camera to see colored objects/walls

**The core loop should be:**
1. Navigate to the good source half (GoToColor BLUE or GREEN)
2. Once on BLACK floor, immediately head back to nest
3. Navigate to nest (GoToColor RED wall)
4. Once on WHITE floor, repeat

**Key issue with my previous designs:** When a robot is at the source doing GoToColor(BLUE), it hits the wall and stays there. It needs a trigger to leave. The trigger should be "on BLACK floor → switch to return mode."

**But wait:** GoToColor(BLUE) will keep robot at the blue wall. The transition "on BLACK floor → go to RED" should fire as soon as the robot enters the source. That's correct!

**Similarly:** GoToColor(RED) keeps robot at the nest wall. The transition "on WHITE floor → go to BLUE/GREEN" fires as soon as it reaches the nest.

So the 2-state machine: `S0: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN), on BLACK → S1; S1: GoToColor(RED), on WHITE → S0` should work perfectly.

The probability parameter for floor conditions (0/1/2) controls how reliably the transition fires. I should use HIGH probabilities (0.99) to make transitions fire immediately.

**Why might this not be working well?** Perhaps the robots start in the middle and the GoToColor behavior can't see the colored walls from far away. In that case, exploration first might help.

**Also:** I should reconsider - maybe the issue is that both BLUE and GREEN walls are visible from the source, and GoToColor picks the nearest. The robot needs to specifically go to ONE half.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback. Let me fundamentally reconsider what might be going wrong.

## Reasoning

**Why might my machines be scoring poorly?**

1. **GoToColor behavior**: It moves toward the nearest object of a given color. From the middle of the arena, can robots see BLUE/GREEN walls? Maybe not if they're far away. The camera range might be limited.

2. **The transition logic issue**: When using `--c0x0 0` (BLACK floor) with probability 0.99 to transition FROM the GoToColor(BLUE) state, the robot transitions as SOON as it touches black floor. But the source area IS the black floor. So the robot enters black, immediately switches to GoToColor(RED), returns to nest. This IS the correct behavior for foraging!

3. **But which color is "good"?** This is the key unknown. My machines try both BLUE and GREEN. One should score well, one badly. The evolutionary process should favor the good one.

4. **Maybe the issue is that robots start in the CENTER and can't see any colored walls initially.** They need exploration first to reach an area where they can see the walls.

5. **Alternative approach**: Use GoAwayColor(RED) to push robots away from nest toward source, then GoToColor(RED) to return. This avoids needing to see specific wall colors.

**New strategy - avoid specific source colors entirely:**
- S0: GoAwayColor(RED) → move toward source
- On BLACK floor → S1 (at source, item picked up)
- S1: GoToColor(RED) → return to nest  
- On WHITE floor → S0

This works regardless of which half is good/bad... wait, no. If we don't target a specific half, robots will randomly pick BLUE or GREEN items. Half will be good, half bad → net score ≈ 0.

**We MUST target a specific half.** The question is which one.

**New insight**: Let me try targeting BOTH within one machine - spend time in BLUE half, return. The evolutionary process will reward the right color. Let me also try machines that go to BLUE first, then if that's bad, GREEN.

**Actually**: In a typical AutoMoDe mission setup, one color is consistently good across all runs. The optimizer should find it. I need to ensure I'm submitting BOTH blue-targeting and green-targeting machines so the optimizer can select the right one.

**Key new idea**: Use `GoAwayColor(GREEN)` to push robots toward the BLUE half, or `GoAwayColor(BLUE)` to push toward GREEN half. This might be more effective than GoToColor for targeting a specific half.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been getting no score feedback for 6 rounds, which means I need to think more carefully about what could be fundamentally wrong with my approach.

## Reasoning

**Deep reconsideration:**

The problem states the score is MINIMISED. The good half gives -1 per delivery, bad half gives +1. With 20 robots over 1200 steps, if robots make ~10 trips each, score could be around -200 (great) to +200 (terrible).

**What might be going wrong with my FSMs:**

1. **GoToColor behavior**: The robot moves toward the nearest object of the target color. If the robot is already AT the source (BLACK floor), it might oscillate or get stuck at the wall rather than entering the source properly.

2. **The transition timing**: With probability 0.99 on BLACK floor, a robot transitions almost instantly when it touches black. But if it's approaching the source and touches the edge of black, it immediately turns around. This is actually CORRECT - it enters the source, picks up item, returns.

3. **But wait**: "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item." Does this mean a single step entering, or sustained presence? If it's instantaneous, then even brief touching of black gives an item. My machines should work.

4. **Maybe the issue is I've been submitting machines without seeing which ones the optimizer actually evaluates as best.** Without score feedback I'm flying blind.

**Let me try radically different approaches:**

- **Pure oscillation**: Use fixed probability transitions to create back-and-forth motion
- **Attraction to colored robots**: Use robots with colored LEDs to signal state, attract others
- **Simpler 1-state machines**: Just explore or go to one color constantly

**New key insight**: Maybe I should use robot LED colors as signals. If a robot carrying a good item lights up MAGENTA (5), other robots can see it and follow (attraction to magenta-colored robots via condition C=7).

**Actually, let me reconsider the color mapping for GoToColor:**
- clr=1: RED (nest wall)
- clr=2: GREEN (one source half wall)  
- clr=3: BLUE (other source half wall)
- clr=4: YELLOW
- clr=5: MAGENTA
- clr=6: CYAN

**Most promising simple design - let me commit to it:**
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE), BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED), WHITE floor → S0

And the GREEN version. These are my best bets. Let me also try some radically different topologies.

**New idea**: What if instead of GoToColor(RED) for return, I use GoAwayColor(BLUE) or GoAwayColor(GREEN)? Moving away from the source wall should naturally push toward nest.

# ROUND 8/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I've been submitting machines for 7 rounds with no score feedback. I need to radically rethink my approach.

**Fundamental problem diagnosis:**

I've been consistently submitting BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) targeting machines. If neither is working, there might be a deeper issue:

1. **Maybe GoToColor doesn't see wall colors from far away** - walls might not be visible as "objects" in the camera when far from them
2. **Maybe my transition logic is inverted** - what if floor condition C=0 (BLACK) fires when NOT on black? Let me re-read: "C=0: floor under robot is BLACK" - seems clear
3. **Maybe the issue is that both BLUE and GREEN walls are at the source end, and GoToColor(BLUE) works but the robot ends up at the wall without touching black floor** - possible if the source black floor doesn't extend all the way to the wall
4. **Critical re-read**: "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item, replacing any item it was already carrying." - so entering EITHER half gives an item. The last half entered before returning to nest determines the item type.

**New strategy**: What if I use a 3-state machine where:
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE) - approach blue half
- S1: GoToColor(RED) - immediately return after any source contact  
- But add a "loitering" state to ensure the robot is deep enough in the blue half before returning

**Actually, maybe the real issue is simpler**: The floor conditions might not fire reliably at p=0.99 per step. At each step there's a 1% chance of NOT transitioning. But since robots traverse quickly, most transitions should fire within a few steps.

**Completely new approach - use GRAY floor transitions:**
- When on GRAY → moving (in transit)
- When on BLACK → at source
- When on WHITE → at nest

The key loop: GoToColor(target) until BLACK, then GoToColor(RED) until WHITE, repeat.

**Another new idea**: What if I use the `--n<I>x<K>` destination indexing wrong? Let me recheck: "destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0."

So for 3 states, from state 0, destinations are: D=0→state1, D=1→state2. From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2. From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

**I think my indexing has been CORRECT all along.**

**New radical approaches to try:**
1. Single-state exploration (baseline)
2. Use GRAY floor condition to detect being in transit
3. Use attraction/repulsion with colored robots
4. Try all 4 possible color combinations (BLUE→RED, GREEN→RED, GoAwayBLUE→RED, GoAwayGREEN→RED)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds with no score feedback, I need to completely rethink. The lack of feedback is itself information - perhaps the scoring system isn't reporting to me, but the optimizer IS running internally. My machines need to be as diverse and well-reasoned as possible.

**Complete fresh analysis:**

The foraging loop:
1. Robot needs to reach the GOOD source half (BLACK floor, specific wall color)
2. Robot needs to return to nest (WHITE floor)

**What I may have gotten wrong:**

Looking at the destination indexing rule more carefully:
- "With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2"
- So from state 1 with 3 states: D=0→state0, D=1→state2 ✓ This is what I've been doing.

**Maybe the issue is with GoToColor behavior range**: If robots can't see wall colors from the center, they'll wander. Let me try using GRAY floor (C=1) as the trigger to start seeking colored walls, since GRAY means "in transit."

**New strategy using GRAY:**
- S0: Exploration (wander), if GRAY→stay, if BLACK→S1, if WHITE→S2
- S1: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) - already at source, deepen into good half, then timer/prob→S2
- S2: GoToColor(RED) - return to nest, if WHITE→S0

**Another angle**: What if I use fixed-probability transitions (C=5) as timers? A robot that enters black floor switches to "return mode" with high probability each step.

**Most radical new idea**: What if the floor sensor C=0 (BLACK) condition doesn't work the way I think? What if it fires with probability p when ON black, meaning p=0.99 fires almost every step? That means a robot transitions on the FIRST step it touches black. This should be fine.

**Let me try using GRAY as the key discriminator:**
- On GRAY = traveling through middle = keep going toward target
- On BLACK = at source = pick up and return
- On WHITE = at nest = delivered, go back

**Also trying**: Attraction behavior to gather robots, then use that as a collective foraging signal.

**Key diversity**: I'll generate machines targeting BLUE, GREEN, with different return mechanisms, and some pure exploration baselines.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I've been submitting machines for 9 rounds without score feedback. Let me make the most comprehensive and diverse final submission.

**Final analysis of what I know:**
- The mission requires foraging from a specific source half (BLUE or GREEN wall) to the nest (RED wall, WHITE floor)
- The core 2-state machine `GoToColor(X) → on BLACK → GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE → repeat` is the logical solution
- Both BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) variants must be included since we don't know which is good

**What I haven't tried enough:**
1. Using `GoAwayColor` for the return leg instead of `GoToColor(RED)` - moving away from BLUE/GREEN pushes toward RED
2. Using GRAY floor as intermediate trigger
3. Machines that don't rely on seeing specific wall colors at all - just floor-color driven oscillation
4. Pure floor-based: GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source, GoToColor(RED) to return - this hits BOTH halves randomly which is neutral
5. Combining transitions: BLACK OR GRAY → return (be more aggressive about returning)

**The simplest possible correct machine:**
- S0: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN), transition on BLACK floor (p=0.99) → S1
- S1: GoToColor(RED), transition on WHITE floor (p=0.99) → S0

I'll submit 6 pairs (BLUE/GREEN variants) of my best designs, emphasizing the core foraging loop with slight variations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
